THIS APPLICATION IS FILED UNDER RULE 9 OF THE COMPANIES
(COURT) RULES, 1959, PRAYING TO PASS AN ORDER SUITABLY
AMENDING THE SCHEME OF COMPROMISE SANCTIONED BY THIS
HON’BLE COURT VIDE ORDER DATED 08.10.2004 PASSED IN
CO.P.NO.37/2003 DIRECTING THE RESPONDENT TO PAY THE AMOUNTS
DUE TO THE APPLICANTS IN AGREED FIVE INSTALLMENTS WITH
INTEREST TILL THE DATE OF RESPECTIVE PAYMENTS INSTEAD OF
INTEREST PAYABLE ONLY UPTO 31.03.2002, ALTERNATIVELY, TO PASS
AN ORDER THAT THE RESPONDENT COMPANY M/S MAHA RASHTRA
APEX CORPORATION LIMITED, BE WOUND UP BY THE COURT UNDER
THE PROVISIONS OF THE COMPANIES ACT, 1956 IN THE INTEREST OF
JUSTICE AND EQUITY.
 THIS APPLICATION COMING ON FOR DICTATING ORDERS, THIS
DAY, ORDER WAS MADE THEREIN AS UNDER:
CORAM: HON'BLE MR JUSTICE S.R.KRISHNA KUMAR

- 232 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
ORAL ORDER
 This C.A.No.564/2011 is filed by Ramachandra Joshi and
others under Rule 9 of the Companies (Court) Rules, 1959 (for
short “the said Rules of 1959”) seeking winding up of the
respondent-Company under Section 392(2) of the Companies Act,
1956 (for short “the said Act of 1956”) and for other reliefs.
2. Heard learned counsel for the applicants and learned
Senior counsel for the respondent-Company and perused the
material on record.
3. A perusal of the material on record will indicate that the
respondent-Company was incorporated in the year 1943 and was
carrying on business as NBFC having 100 branches and 350 sub-offices across the country. The respondent-Company filed COP
No.37/2003 on 21.02.2003 under Section 391 to 393 and 394A of
the said Act of 1956 seeking sanction of the Scheme of
Compromise and Arrangement (for short “the Scheme”) with the
appointed date being 01.04.2002 and effective date as 15.12.2004.
By order dated 08.10.2004, this Court sanctioned the Scheme
subject to compliance of conditions enumerated in paragraph 63 of
its order.

- 233 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
4. Subsequently, the applicants, who are depositors have
filed the instant application seeking winding up on the ground that
the terms and conditions stipulated by this Court in the aforesaid
order dated 08.10.2004 and subsequent orders had not been
complied with and the depositors had not been paid monies due to
them warranting winding up of the Company under Section 392(2)
of the said Act of 1956. It is contended that the dues under the
Scheme were not cleared, periodical statements had not been filed
to show the financial status of the Company and the Scheme is a
contract between the parties and cannot be modified without the
consent of each of the creditors/depositors and as such, the
application deserves to be allowed.
4.1. In support of his submissions, he places reliance upon
the following judgments:
i) S.K. Gupta and another Vs. K.P Jain and another –
(1979) 3 SCC 54.
ii) M/s. Meghal Homes Private Limited Vs. Shree Nivas
Girni K K Samithi and others – (2007) AIR SCW 5558.
iii) Bakshi Security and Personnel Services Private
Limited Vs. Dev Kishan Computed Private Limited and
others – (2016) 8 SCC 446.
5. The respondent-Company has opposed the said
application inter alia contending that the application was not

- 234 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
maintainable and that the payment and dues under the Scheme, in
particular, the applicants had been cleared and annual reports,
affidavits and documents had been filed regularly by the
respondent-Company, who has been taking necessary steps to
comply with the Scheme, which has been substantially complied
with and as such, the question of winding up of the Company would
not arise in the facts and circumstances of the case and as such,
the application is liable to be dismissed.
5.1. In support of his submissions, he places reliance upon
the following judgments:
i) S.K Gupta & anr. Vs. K.P Jain & anr. - (1979) 3 SCC 54.
ii) My Palace Mutually Aided Co-operative Society Vs. B.
Mahesh and others – (2022) SCC OnLine SC 1063.
iii) Durgesh Sharma Vs. Jayshree – (2008) 9 SCC 648.
iv) Ramprakash Agarwal Vs. Gopi Krishnan –
(2013) 11 SCC 296.
v) IBA Health (I) Private Limited Vs. M/s. Infodrive
Systems – (2010) 10 SCC 553.
vi) National Small Industries Corporation Limited Vs.
Industrial Metal Works (P) Ltd – (1984) SCComp Case
14 (Cal).
vii) Mansukhlal Vs. M V Shah – (1976) GLR 592.
viii) Deepika Chit Fund Private Ltd Vs. Secretary – 2012
SCC OnLine AP 241.

- 235 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
6. I have given my anxious consideration to the rival
submissions and perused the material on record.
7. A perusal of the material on record will indicate that in
all 46 applications have been filed seeking directions subsequent to
the order dated 08.10.2004 passed by this Court. Out of the 46
applications, payment has been made in full in 45 applications and
proof of payment has been produced by the Company along with
memos under such applications and in several cases, payment has
been recorded by this Court from time to time. It is also seen that
in so far as the applicants in the instant C.A.No.564/2011 are
concerned, many applicants have already been paid and the same
has been recorded by this Court in its order dated 08.08.2018. In
my considered opinion, the instant application in C.A.No.564/2011
is liable to be dismissed for the following reasons:
(i) The instant application has been filed under Rule 9 of the
said Rules of 1959, which are akin to Section 151 CPC and the
said Rule cannot be invoked in view of the specific provision
contained in Section 392(2) of the said Act of 1956, which
contemplates that in the event of impossibility in the working of the
Sanction Scheme with or without modification and in the light of this

- 236 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
express provision, applicants are not entitled to invoke Rule 9 and
consequently, the application is not maintainable and liable to be
dismissed. In this context, it is relevant to state that in My Palace
Mutually Aided Co-operative Society’s case (supra), the Apex
Court held as under:
 “26. Section 151 of the CPC provides for Civil Courts
to invoke their inherent jurisdiction and utilize the same to
meet the ends of justice or to prevent abuse of process.
Although such a provision is worded broadly, this Court has
tempered the provision to limit its ambit to only those
circumstances where certain procedural gaps exist, to
ensure that substantive justice is not obliterated by hyper
technicalities. As far back as in 1961, this Court in Padam
Sen v. State of U.P., AIR 1961 SC 218, observed as under:
“8. …The inherent powers of the Court are in addition
to the powers specifically conferred on the Court by
the Code. They are complementary to those powers
and therefore it must be held that the Court is free to
exercise them for the purposes mentioned in Section
151 of the Code when the exercise of those powers
is not in any way in conflict with what has been
expressly provided in the Code or against the
intentions of the Legislature. It is also well recognized
that the inherent power is not to be exercised in a
manner which will be contrary to or different from the
procedure expressly provided in the Code.”
27. In exercising powers under Section 151 of the
CPC, it cannot be said that the civil courts can exercise
substantive jurisdiction to unsettle already decided issues. A
Court having jurisdiction over the relevant subject matter has
the power to decide and may come either to a right or a

- 237 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
wrong conclusion. Even if a wrong conclusion is arrived at or
an incorrect decree is passed by the jurisdictional court, the
same is binding on the parties until it is set aside by an
appellate court or through other remedies provided in law.
28. Section 151 of the CPC can only be applicable if
there is no alternate remedy available in accordance with the
existing provisions of law. Such inherent power cannot
override statutory prohibitions or create remedies which are
not contemplated under the Code. Section 151 cannot be
invoked as an alternative to filing fresh suits, appeals,
revisions, or reviews. A party cannot find solace in Section
151 to allege and rectify historic wrongs and bypass
procedural safeguards inbuilt in the CPC.”
 So also, in Durgesh Sharma’s case (supra), the Apex
Court held as under:
 “56. We are unable to agree with the view that in such
cases, inherent powers may be exercised under Section 151
of the Code as held by the High Court of Punjab and
Haryana in SBI. It is settled law that inherent powers may be
exercised ex debito justitiae in those cases, where there is
no express provision in the Code. The said power cannot be
exercised in contravention or in conflict of or ignoring
express and specific provisions of law. Since the law relating
to transfer is contained in Sections 22 to 25 of the Code, and
they are exhaustive in nature, Section 151 has no
application. Even that contention, therefore, cannot take the
case of the respondent wife further.”

- 238 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
 Further, in Ramprakash Agarwal’s case (supra), the Apex
Court held as under:
 “13. Section 151 CPC is not a substantive provisions
that confers the right to get any relief of any kind. It is a mere
procedural provision which enable a party to have the
proceedings of a pending suit conducted in a manner that is
consistent with justice and equity. The court can do justice
between the parties before it. Similarly, inherent powers
cannot be used to re-open settled matters. The inherent
powers of the Court must, to that extent, be regarded as
abrogated by the legislature. A provision barring the exercise
of inherent power need not be express, it may even be
implied. Inherent power cannot be used to restrain the
execution of a decree at the instance of one who was not a
party to suit. Such power is absolutely essential for securing
the ends of justice, and to overcome the failure of justice.
The Court under Section 151 CPC may adopt any procedure
to do justice, unless the same is expressly prohibited.”
(ii) A perusal of the instant application will indicate that
though the same is filed by 1916 applicants, the affidavit in support
of the joint/common application has been filed by applicant No.1,
Ramachandra Joshi, who has since collected/received the amount
due to him as recorded in the order dated 08.08.2018 passed by
this Court; further, upon his demise, no additional affidavits or
memos of calculation, as directed by this Court in its order dated

- 239 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
08.08.2018, have been filed by the remaining applicants, who have
not subsequently filed any pleadings or documents before this
Court and on this ground also, the application seeking winding up
is liable to be dismissed.
(iii) A perusal of the instant application filed by 1916
applicants will indicate that the dues are claimed in relation to 4783
deposit certificates, out of which, dues with respect of 3747 deposit
certificates have been cleared as evidenced by the documents
produced along with the memos dated 05.11.2018 and 27.11.2018
and the cheques issued to holders of 206 deposit certificates are
untraceable and there is ambiguity and no clarity with regard to 155
deposit certificates in addition to the fact that 66 deposit certificates
have been repeated in the application. It is also pertinent to note
that the respondent-Company has deposited a sum of
Rs.13,95,74,920/- before this Court, which includes/covers the
principal amount and accrued interest due to the holders of the
aforesaid 206 and 155 deposit certificates, in respect of whom
paper publication was taken out and issued, as can be seen from
the memo dated 12.06.2019 and by opening 6 front offices in
Bengaluru, Mangaluru, Dharawad, Udupi, Davanagere and Mysuru
as is clear from the affidavit dated 12.07.2018 and also sending

- 240 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
individual notices through registered post as per the memos filed
by the respondent. Under these circumstances also, the question
of winding up of the respondent-Company would not arise in the
facts and circumstances of the instant case.
(iv) A perusal of the material on record will indicate that at
the time of approval of the scheme, the respondent – Company
was due to pay a sum of approximately Rs.361.81 Crores and
subsequently, as on 30.09.2023, the total amount payable by the
respondent-Company has been reduced, who only took Rs.30.73
Crores, as can be seen from the certificate issued by the Chartered
Accountant; further, the respondent-Company has already settled
the amounts due to the depositors in public, who are traceable and
have surrendered their deposit certificates and as stated supra, the
respondent has already deposited a total sum of Rs.13,95,74,920/-,
which covers the sum of Rs.13.96 Crores payable to them towards
principal amount and accrued interest, as referred to in the orders
dated 11.04.2023, 31.05.2023, 22.09.2023 and 24.11.2023 passed
by this Court, which indicates that there is no delay attributable to
the Company in making payment; further, the affiliates of the
respondent-Company have passed Board Resolution dated
31.10.2023 and 22.11.2023 waiving the requirement to pay

- 241 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
delayed period interest, thereby assuring the Company that they
will not demand the same. It is also significant to note that the
respondent has continuously filed affidavits, memos, statements
etc., from 26.02.2008 onwards before this Court, which is sufficient
to come to the conclusion that the various allegations made by the
applicant in this regard cannot be accepted. Under these
circumstances, the present application filed by the applicants is
liable to be dismissed on this ground also.
(v) Section 392(1)(a) and (b) of the said Act of 1956
contemplates that this Court has the power to supervise and modify
the Scheme. Section 392(2) provides that a Company can be
wound up only if the Scheme of Compromise despite modification
cannot be worked out satisfactorily and such winding up if ordered,
shall be deemed to be winding up under Section 433 of the said
Act of 1956. As stated supra, the respondent – Company has
cleared the entire liability under the Scheme except delayed period
interest to unclaimed depositors and the delay, if any, in payment
under the scheme was due to reasons beyond the control of the
Company; in this regard, this Court permitted the Company to sell
its assets as can be seen from the order dated 08.10.2004 and
various orders up to 22.09.2023, all of which indicates that this

- 242 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
Court has permitted the Company to comply with the Scheme in
the process of supervising the scheme; so also, the efforts of the
Company to notify the untraceable depositors have proved futile,
apart from the fact that more than 100 cases having initiated by the
respondent-Company against its debtors. In view of these facts
and circumstances, it cannot be said that the applicant has made
out valid or sufficient ground to seek winding up of the respondent-Company and the claim of the applicant cannot be accepted.
(vi) In S.K. Gupta’s case (supra), the Apex Court clearly
held that winding up is the last/ultimate resort, which can be
resorted to only if modification of the Scheme of Compromise was
not possible and in the event there are unforeseen obstacles /
impediments that may be faced by the Company while
implementing a complex scheme spread over several years, power
of widest amplitude has been conferred upon this Court to modify
the Scheme; in the said judgment it was held as under:
“12. Section 391 envisages a compromise or
arrangement being proposed for consideration by members
and/or creditors of a company liable to be wound up under
the Companies Act, 1956. Compromise or arrangement has
to be between creditors and/or members of the company and
the company, as the case may be. It was always open to the
company to offer a compromise to any of the creditors or

- 243 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
enter into arrangement with each of the members. The
scheme in this case is essentially a compromise between the
company and its unsecured creditors. The scheme when
sanctioned does not merely operate as an agreement
between the parties but has statutory force and is binding not
only on the company but even dissenting creditors or
members, as the case may be. The effect of the sanctioned
scheme is “to supply by recourse to the procedure thereby
prescribed the absence of that individual agreement by every
member of the class to be bound by the scheme which would
otherwise be necessary to give it validity” [see J.K. (Bombay)
Pvt. Ltd. v. New Kaiser-i-Hind Spg. & Wvg. Co. Ltd. [AIR
1970 SC 1041 : (1969) 2 SCR 866, 891 : (1970) 40 Com Cas
689] ]. Further Section 391(1) itself, by a specific and positive
provision, prescribes who can move an application under it.
Only the creditor or member of that company or a liquidator
in the case of a company being wound up is entitled to move
an application proposing a compromise or arrangement. By
necessary implication any one other than those specified in
the section would not be entitled to move such an
application.
13. When a scheme is being considered by the Court,
in all its ramifications, for according its sanction, it would not
be possible to comprehend all situations, eventualities and
exigencies that may arise while implementing the scheme.
When a detailed compromise and/or arrangement is worked
out, hitches and impediments may arise and if there was no
provision like the one in Section 392, the only obvious
alternative would be to follow the cumbersome procedure as
provided in Section 391(1) viz. again by approaching the

- 244 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
class of creditors or members to whom the compromise and/
or arrangement was offered to accord their sanction to the
steps to be taken for removing such hitches and
impediments. This would be unduly cumbersome and time-consuming and, therefore, the legislature in its wisdom
conferred power of widest amplitude on the High Court under
Section 392 not only to give directions but to make such
modification in the compromise and/ or arrangement as the
Court may consider necessary, the only limit on the power of
the Court being that such directions can be given and
modifications can be made for the proper working of the
compromise and/or arrangement. The purpose underlying
Section 392 is to provide for effective working of the
compromise and/or arrangement once sanctioned and over
which the Court must exercise continuous supervision [see
Section 392(1)], and if over a period there may arise
obstacles, difficulties or impediments, to remove them, again,
not for any other purpose but for the proper working of the
compromise and/or arrangement. This power either to give
directions to overcome the difficulties or if the provisions of
the scheme themselves create an impediment, to modify the
provision to the extent necessary, can only be exercised so
as to provide for smooth working of the compromise and/or
arrangement. To effectuate this purpose the power of widest
amplitude has been conferred on the High Court and this is a
basic departure from the scheme of the U.K. Act in which
provision analogous to Section 392 is absent. The sponsors
of the scheme under Section 206 of the U.K. Act have tried
to get over the difficulty by taking power in the scheme of
compromise or arrangement to make alterations and

- 245 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
modifications as proposed by the Court. But the legislature,
foreseeing that a complex or complicated scheme of
compromise or arrangement spread over a long period may
face unforeseen and unanticipated obstacles, has conferred
power of widest amplitude on the Court to give directions
and, if necessary, to modify the scheme for the proper
working of the compromise or arrangement. The only
limitation on the power of the Court, as already mentioned, is
that all such directions that the Court may consider
appropriate to give or make such modifications in the
scheme, must be for the proper working of the compromise
and/or arrangement.
16. If the Court can suo motu act, it is immaterial as to
who drew the attention of the Court to a situation which
necessitated Court's intervention. Where the power is
conferred on the Court to take action on its own motion the
information emanating from whatever source which calls for
Court's attention can as well be obtained from any person
without questioning his credentials, moving an application
drawing attention of the Court to a situation where it must
act. Undoubtedly, the Court may decline to act at the
instance of a busy body but if the action proposed to be
taken is justified, valid, legal or called for, the capacity or
credentials of the person who brought the situation calling for
Court's intervention is hardly relevant nor would it invalidate
the resultant action only on that ground. Therefore, when
sub-section (2) confers power on the Court to act on its own
motion, the question of locus standi hardly arises. The High
Court while examining the question of locus standi, after
combing the provision contained in sub-section (2), wholly

- 246 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
overlooked the important provision therein contained that the
High Court can act on its own motion. It was, however, said
in passing that sub-section (2) enables the Court to wind up
the company and, therefore, the Court may act on its own
motion or on the application of any person interested in the
affairs of the company not for modifying the scheme or for
any directions but for winding-up the company. But when the
Court is required to act under Section 392(1), the limitations
and restrictions imposed upon the Court under Section
391(1) must be read in Section 392(1) because the sections
are complementary to each other. This submission overlooks
the two different stages at which Sections 391 and 392
operate though they may be complementary to each other.
Two sub-sections of Section 392 have to be harmoniously
read and sub-section (2) clearly indicates the power of Court
to take action suo motu while taking action under sub-section
(1). Again this approach is inconsistent with the language
employed in Section 392(2) in that the Court can wind up the
company under Section 392(2) if and only if it is satisfied that
the compromise and/or arrangement sanctioned by it cannot
be satisfactorily worked with or without modifications. The
Court has to reach an affirmative conclusion before acting
under Section 392(2) that the compromise and/or
arrangement cannot be worked satisfactorily with or without
modification [see J.K. (Bombay) P. Ltd.]. It follows as a
corollary that if the compromise or arrangement can be
worked as it is or by making modifications, the Court will
have no power to wind up the company under Section
392(2). Now, if the arrangement or compromise can be
worked with or without modification, the Court must

- 247 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
undertake the exercise to find out what modifications are
necessary to make the compromise or arrangement
workable and that it can do so on its own motion or on the
application of any person interested in the affairs of the
company. If such be the power conferred on the Court, it is
difficult to entertain the submission that an application for
directions or modification cannot be entertained except when
made by a member or creditor. It would whittle down the
power of the Court in that it cannot do so on its own motion.
18. Assuming that the Court would not act on its own,
the next question is : could it act under Section 392(1) on the
application of any person interested in the affairs of the
company? Now, if the Court under Section 392(2) can order
winding-up of the company on the application of any person
interested in the affairs of the company who need not be a
member or a creditor, we fail to see how the Court cannot act
on the application of such a person interested in the affairs of
the company either to give directions or to make
modifications so as to make the compromise or arrangement
workable. Winding up meaning civil death of a company,
must be the ultimate resort of the Court. A living workable
scheme infusing life into a sick unit is generally to be
preferred to civil death of the company. There is, therefore,
no warrant for circumscribing the expression “on the
application of any person interested in the affairs of the
company” as to limit it to member or creditor. If the
legislature used the expression “member or creditor” in
Section 391(1) and yet used an expression of wider
denotation “any person interested in the affairs of the
company”, the legislative intention is clearly exposed in that

- 248 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
any such person interested in the affairs of the company
need not be limited or restricted to refer to a member or
creditor. It would, therefore, be necessary to ascertain
whether the appellant would be comprehended in the
expression “any person interested in the affairs of the
company”.”
(vii) The material on record also discloses that there is a
serious and bonafide dispute with regard to the debt/payment said
to be due from the respondent-Company and under these
circumstances, an order of winding up cannot be passed as held by
the Apex Court in IBA Health (I) Pvt. Ltd’s case (supra), wherein
it is held as under:
 “31. Where the company has a bona fide dispute, the
petitioner cannot be regarded as a creditor of the company
for the purpose of winding up. “Bonafide dispute” implies the
existence of a substantial ground for the dispute raised.
Where the Company Court is satisfied that a debt upon
which a petition is founded is hotly contested debt and also
doubtful, the Company Court should not entertain such a
petition. The Company Court is expected to go into the
causes of refusal by the Company to pay before coming to
that conclusion. The Company Court is expected to
ascertain that the company’s refusal is supported by a
reasonable cause or a bonafide dispute in which the dispute
can only be adjudicated by a trial in a civil court.

- 249 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
32. In the instant case, the Company Court was very
causal in its approach and did not make any endeavour to
ascertain as to whether the company sought to be wound up
for non-payment of debt has a defence which is substantial
in nature and if not adjudicated in a proper forum, would
cause serious prejudice to the company.”
 It is also relevant to state that in the absence of the
applicant producing calculation to indicate non-payment of dues,
which are seriously disputed, there cannot be winding up of the
Company as held by the Calcutta High Court in National Small
Industries Corporation Limited’s Case (supra); as stated earlier,
majority of the dues and directions issued by this Court have been
complied with and the applicants, who have approached this Court
have either received the amount due to them or are not interested
in pursuing their claims or have remained untraceable, despite
efforts being made by the respondent coupled with the remaining
dues being seriously disputed by the Company and consequently,
the application is liable to be dismissed on this ground also.
(viii) In Mansukh Lal’s case (supra), the Gujarath High
Court held that though the Scheme of Compromise is a contract,
power of the widest amplitude has been conferred upon this Court
to modify the scheme without the consent of the creditors as under:

- 250 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003

“9. The next question, however, is whether every such
change in sponsorship should be referred back to the
members and creditors. No hard and fast rule can be laid
down on this point. In England, a court practice has been
developed to circumvent the situation. In England, the
sponsors of the scheme also take the power in the scheme
itself to accept the modifications if proposed or suggested or
indicated by the court while sanctioning the scheme and they
could accept the modification, armed with this power. That
would avoid going back to the creditors and members on
every occasion when some modification becomes necessary
or some modification is suggested by the court before the
court is prepared to put its stamp of approval on the scheme.
The framers of the company law in India have conferred
statutory powers on the High Court to make such
modifications in the compromise or arrangement as the court
may consider necessary for the proper working of the
compromise and arrangement. The power of the widest
amplitude has been conferred on the court under section
392(1)(b) and the width and the magnitude of the power can
be gauged from the language employed in section 392(1)(a)
which confers a sort of a supervisory rule on the court during
the period the scheme of compromise or arrange merit is
being implemented. Reading clauses (a) and (b) of sub-section (1) of section 392, it appears that Parliament did not
want the court to be functus officio as soon as the scheme of
compromise and arrangement is sanctioned by it. The court
has a continuing supervision over the implementation of

- 251 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
compromise and arrangement. Unenvisaged, unanticipated,
unforeseen or even unimaginable hitches, obstruction and
impediments may arise in the course of implementation of a
scheme of compromise and arrangement and if on every
such occasion, sponsors have to go back to the parties
concerned for seeking their approval for a modification and
then seek the approval of the court, it would be a long-drawn
out, protracted, time-consuming process with no guarantee
of result and the whole scheme of compromise and
arrangement may be mutilated in the process. Parliament
has, therefore, thought it fit to trust the wisdom of the court
rather than go back to the interested parties. If the parties
have several times to decide the modification with the
democratic process, the good part of an election machinery
apart, the dirt may step in, the conflicting interests may be
bought and sold, and, in the process, the whole scheme of
compromise and arrangement may be so twisted and torn
out of context as to be thoroughly useless and may be
jettisoned. In order, therefore, to guard against this
eventuality and situation, which is clearly envisageable,
Parliament has conferred power on the court, not only to
make modifications even at the time of sanctioning the
scheme, but at any time thereafter during the period the
scheme is being implemented. Conceding that, before the
court sanctions the scheme, it partakes the character of an
emerging contract between the company and the creditors
and members; once the court approves it, it becomes a
statutorily enforceable contract even on dissidents, with
power in the court to modify, amend or correct or revise the
contract the outer periphery or its limit on the power being

- 252 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
that, after testing it on the anvil of probabilities, surrounding
circumstances and the prevalent state of affairs, it can be
done for the proper working of the compromise and
arrangement, and, subject to this limit on the court's power,
the power seems to be absolute and of the widest amplitude
and it would be unwise to curtail it by process of
interpretation. Mr. S.B. Majumdar in this connection drew my
attention to J.K. (Bombay) Private Ltd. v. New Kaiser-I-Hind
Spinning and Weaving Co. Ltd. [[1970] 40 Comp Cas 689,
706 (SC).] , wherein it is observed as under:
“Under section 392 of the Act the High Court
which has sanctioned the scheme has the power to
supervise the carrying out of it and to give
directions in regard to any matter or to make
modifications in it as it may consider necessary for
its proper working. But if the court is satisfied that
the scheme cannot be worked satisfactorily with or
without modifications it can either suo moto or on
an application by any person interested in the
company's affairs order its winding-up.”
10. It would thus appear that till the court believes that
some modifications are necessary for the effective
implementation of the scheme, there is no fetter on its power
in making the modifications. It is true that the court may not
be inclined to undertake responsibility of making
modifications which would vitally affect different interests
affected by the modifications and may have recourse to
ascertain their views or may permit them to express their
opinion either at a meeting or by any other suitable method,
but that is more an act of wisdom rather than a fetter on the
power of the court. The court's power, subject to the
limitation placed by the section, appears to be absolute.”

- 253 -
NC: 2024:KHC:36160
CA No. 564 of 2011
IN COP No.37 of 2003
(ix) A perusal of the continuous proceedings recorded by
this Court in several orders from 08.10.2004, till date is sufficient to
come to the conclusion that the applicants have not made out valid
or sufficient ground to enable them to seek winding up of the
Company by invoking the provisions contained in Section 392 (2) of
the said Act of 1956 and consequently, I am of the considered
opinion that the instant application in C.A.No.564/2011 is devoid of
merit and the same is liable to be dismissed.
 Accordingly, C.A.No.564/2011 is dismissed.
Sd/-
(S.R.KRISHNA KUMAR)
JUDGE
Srl./BMC
